@@8582977 <h> Quantifying the risks <p> When our great-grandchildren look back on our stewardship of the planet , they may well struggle to understand why we left it so late , and did so little , to avert the dislocations they will have to live with . <p> But there is one group of people that will escape their judgement : the climate scientists who have done such a magnificent job over the past few decades of warning us of the risks we are running . <p> This month has seen the publication of a report from the Royal Society on Implications of Climate Change for New Zealand . <p> I imagine young minds reading this in a century 's time and staring in wonder that " they saw it coming and still they did n't stop it ' ' . <p> The report highlights six key risks . <p> For each it asks a few key questions : What is already happening ? <p> What might the future hold ? <p> What would be the implications for us if we manage to achieve a low-carbon future ? <p> @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ knowledge gaps that we need to fill ? <p> ONE <p> Risk one is our exposure to coastal change : rising sea levels , increased frequency and severity of storm surges , flooding , and salination of water tables . <p> All of these will affect east coast communities such as Dunedin more sharply . <p> In a high-carbon world , sea levels would rise by up to a metre by the end of the century , and keep rising after that ; and today 's one-in-100-year high tides will occur " almost daily ' ' . <p> Considerable infrastructure , both public and private , will be affected and " landward migration ' ' will be the unwelcome result . <p> TWO <p> Risk two is flooding . <p> A warmer world is a wetter world and the volume of rainwater during storms will be beyond the capacity of current stormwater systems . <p> Saturated land will see runoff swell rivers and increase the frequency and magnitude of floods with " widespread impacts to property and assets ' ' . <p> THREE <p> Risk three is to fresh water . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ will be more frequent and more severe . <p> The risk of fires will rise . <p> Competition for water will increase for agriculture , town supply and hydro power . <p> Regimes of water management will have to change to allocate supply across competing needs . <p> For some regions , this may result in " transformational changes in farm and land management ' ' . <p> Water may increasingly be priced , and recycled . <p> FOUR <p> Risk four is to our surrounding ocean . <p> New Zealand has an exclusive economic zone of 4million square kilometres , the fourth largest in the world . <p> In a high carbon world , the oceans will become warmer and more acidic . <p> Fish species may migrate south from lower latitudes ( and with them , I would add , the fishing fleets that feed off them ) . <p> Paua , mussels and other marine organisms that form shells will struggle , including some types of plankton upon which extensive food chains rely . <p> FIVE <p> Risk five is to our ecosystems . <p> Some 50,000 species @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Earth . <p> The most severe immediate impacts of climate change on biodiversity are likely to come from human responses to the challenge : new diseases ; more weeds , pests , fires ; less water ; changes to land use and coastal land ; renewable energy systems ( hydro , biomass , wind and marine ) . <p> SIX <p> Risk six is international . Climate change , particularly in a high-carbon scenario , will have widespread and dramatic impacts on the world 's productive , economic , political and social systems . <p> Feeding a 2050 population of nine billion people will require a 70% increase in food production from a world in which the productive potential of many regions will be severely disrupted . <p> Migrations will increase in scale and frequency . <p> The economic viability of some nation states will be undermined . <p> The risks of violent conflicts will increase . <p> New Zealand will be less likely than most to experience these effects directly , but as a trading nation that has so much of what the rest of the world will increasingly need @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ pressures building up in the world outside will surely find their way to our shores also . <p> All of this sounds quite apocalyptic , and it is natural for us to be sceptical of doomsayers . <p> Few people alive today in New Zealand have experienced the collapse of a world they knew and kept them safe . <p> Radical change on the scale being described is perhaps just not something we can imagine . <p> But if we were to ask Dunedin 's newest residents just arrived from Syria , they would tell us how quickly human institutions can crumble under pressure . <p> Believe it or not , I am an optimist . <p> I think humanity will avoid the worst that climate change can do . <p> But only if we are brave enough to recognise the risks we run . <p> Just maybe , our great-grandchildren will be grateful . <p> What do you reckon ? <p> - Colin Campbell-Hunt is an emeritus professor at the CSAFE Centre for Sustainability , University of Otago . Each week in this column , one of a panel of @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ of renewables in electricity generation to 90% by 2025Promote energy-efficient appliances and remove least efficient from the marketUse best practice approaches to reduce agriculture emissions <p> * Incorporate more wood in building ( lower carbon than steel and concrete ) , improve insulationIncrease the focus on energy efficiency in industrial processes <p> * Reform emissions trading and pricing <p> - From " Transition to a low-carbon economy for New Zealand ' ' . For more go to royalsociety.org.nz. 
@@8582978 <h> Climate change fires up again <p> It is said , timing is everything . It is something of a surprise then , that a week after New Zealand ratified the global Paris climate change accord in New York , it is announced the life of the country 's last two coal-fired power generators at Genesis Energy 's Huntly Power Station will be extended by four years . <p> It 's hardly a good look , which explains the positive focus put on the announcement by 51% Government-owned Genesis Energy and Energy and Resources Minister Simon Bridges . <p> Both are lauding the " continuing commitment to phasing out its two remaining coal units by 2022 ' ' . <p> Lest we forget , last year Genesis said it would phase out the two coal units by the end of 2018 , a move which gave the country some much-needed - and internationally noted - climate-change credentials . <p> So why the brakes ? <p> The delay ( for the minister and generator are adamant it is not a U-turn ) is , according to Mr Bridges , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ agreed to by Genesis and the other electricity generators as a result of concern about dry years , changes to the electricity market , the time required to develop new generation and the ongoing uncertainty about the future of the electricity-intensive Tiwai Point aluminium smelter . <p> All are undisputed sticking points , and the market has reacted positively to the announcement of the four-year reprieve . <p> For while the country does have good renewable energy sources ( and the Government maintains it is still on track to meets its 2025 target of 90% renewable electricity generation ) , it does still need the coal-fired back-up in dry years when hydro-lakes are low . <p> Tiwai Point is the sector 's elephant in the room , however . <p> Were it to close , and its demand cease , the market would be flooded with electricity . <p> At the moment , thanks to a Government/Meridian Energy deal , the business , jobs and electricity demand are safeguarded . <p> But , given the volatile aluminium market and climate change and emissions commitments , its fate is far from certain @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ choice but to maintain the status quo at Huntly for the short term . <p> But that wo n't solve the pressing climate-change issue for the long term , or help with the country 's also short-term emissions reduction commitment of 30% below 2005 levels by 2030 . <p> It is true New Zealand is a low-emitter on a global scale , but that is no reason to be complacent . <p> On a per-capita basis we are actually a high emitter , and we can not expect other countries to make genuine reductions if we do not . <p> And we need others to make significant reductions . <p> As an island nation , reliant on agriculture , we have a lot to lose from the effects of climate change . <p> So although electricity generation makes up only 6% of this country 's carbon emissions , as long as the Government steadfastly refuses to take agriculture ( which accounts for almost half our total emissions ) to task , the energy and transport sectors ( which together account for 40% of emissions ) will pay the price . <p> @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ through the likes of petrol prices , road taxes and higher electricity charges . <p> The environment and economy are inextricably entwined , and balancing both is essential for sustainable progress . <p> There must be more collaboration between ministries around Tiwai 's long-term future , and a more urgent focus on new renewable energy , which can provide new jobs and energy security and reduce our carbon footprint . <p> It is still unsettling to hear Government and industry leaders talk about waiting for the development of and demand for new technologies , before we can really speed up our emissions reductions , when the need for action on climate change has been discussed for more than 20 years . <p> Short-term solutions are of little use when it 's a case of now or never . 
@@8583030 <h> Editorial : Govt made the right call by keeping Kiwirail <p> Kiwirail floated the possibility of closing the national rail network . <p> OPINION : Kiwirail backed the idea of shutting down the railways . The turkey voted for Christmas ? . <p> There was no way for the national rail network to spin a profit in the next ten years , and so the " highest value " commercial option would be to shut it all down , except for Auckland and Wellington passenger services , the Kiwirail board concluded . <p> This offer followed an exhaustive nine-month review of possible futures for the rail business , conducted in 2014 but released last week . <p> Perhaps it was a sober assessment of the economics of a challenging industry . Or perhaps Kiwirail bosses were sick of the Government 's indecisive , short-term funding of the state-owned enterprise , and wanted to force a decision on its future . <p> If it was the latter , it worked , at least partially . The Government rejected the idea of shutting down the rail network , as @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ including retrenching to an upper North Island triangle . <p> It also rejected a Treasury preference that Kiwirail be given a single year 's funding while a more comprehensive look at how it might be terminated took place . ( Treasury , after all , has already concluded that the rail network costs more than it benefits the country , even accounting for environmental and safety considerations ) . <p> Instead , the Government decided it still wanted a national rail network . <p> It did n't agree to much greater funding certainty -- it confirmed it for the next two years , instead of the customary one . But that was still momentous for a Government that has often been critical of Labour 's decision to buy the rail business , to the point of calling it near " worthless " . Gone is the 2010 " turnaround " plan ; now there is a more realistic commitment to maintaining the service . <p> Finance Minister Bill English hinted at much of this last year when he said rail would cost " a couple of hundred million dollars every year @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ It wants the ... Government to own a rail company and to have rail , because they regard the alternatives as putting too many trucks on the road . " <p> And , contrary to the Treasury perspective , there are good reasons for why the public 's right . In the age of climate change , putting another 1.2 million trucks on the road each year , as closure would require , seems daft . <p> There is also a widespread suspicion that economic benchmarks for the railways are qualitatively different to those applied to roads -- which are not required to spin a profit , and have often been approved by the current Government despite very dubious cost-benefit ratios . <p> The public might be justifiably suspicious , too , of even parts of the network returning to private hands . Much of Kiwirail 's recent capital expenditure has simply been making up for gaps that opened up in the years before it was returned to public ownership -- a kind of " harvesting " of the railways for private gain . <p> Ultimately , Kiwirail 's future will @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ commodity markets and the pace and nature of new technologies . <p> But it would have been a pinched and premature decision to close the national rail network down . The Government made the wiser call .

@@8585441 <h> Elderly woman 's death reminder to check smoke alarms <p> Elderly woman 's death reminder to check smoke alarms , and get out fast . <p> Firefighters say the death of a 79-year old Whanganui woman following a house fire , last Thursday 22 April , could have been prevented with a working smoke alarm . <p> The woman was rescued by firefighters and taken to hospital in a critical condition after being revived at the scene , but subsequently died from her injuries . <p> 18 people have now been killed in avoidable house fires since July 2015 . <p> Whanganui Fire Service area commander Bernie Rush says a smoke alarm was located in the hallway but it did not contain a battery . <p> " It 's extremely disappointing , given two older women died in a similar way in 2014 . Unfortunately history has repeated itself . " <p> Mr Rush says the woman called 111 after discovering the fire , which is believed to have been caused by an electric blanket in her bedroom . <p> " We strongly advise people to get @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ help from a safe place nearby , because house fires can kill in minutes . The smoke is extremely toxic and can overwhelm people in seconds . " <p> He says a working smoke alarm would have given her more warning and more time to escape . <p> The Fire Service recommends people install photo-electric smoke alarms with a built-in long-life battery . They are particularly useful for rental properties because the batteries ca n't be removed and do n't need to be replaced . Long-life photo-electric smoke alarms are also cheaper in the long run , because they last for up to 10 years . <p> Mr Rush also says electric blankets can occasionally cause fires , so they should be replaced every five years with newer heat-protected models , which are safer . <h> Parliament Today : <p> Last year , the world began spending more money on weapons again , for the first time since 2011 ... " <p> TPP-Free-Wellington calls for supporters to rally this Wednesday 4 May , at 12 noon at Parliament , to support this call for a binding referendum . " <p> " We 're calling for a binding referendum because TPP would significantly undermine our elected representatives ' ability and freedom to work for us , only to give more power to foreign " investors " to secure their " property " and profits , and to offshore tribunals to enforce the new regime . @@8589257 <h> Know your media <p> I 'd like to thank the summit 's organisers for today 's timely and important topic : I Know What the Media Tells You - But Do You Know Who I Am ? <p> Most of us of course Maori New Zealanders : members of these communities regularly tell us that the media too often misrepresents , sensationalises or fails to include their voices in news stories about them . <p> Often news stories about ethnic minorities have negative themes and present minorities as problems and not as people . <p> This is not a new phenomenon and with the advent of social media , commented we contacted the trust : who were stunned . <p> They 'd been told the paper was working on a positive feature story about how diverse Auckland celebrated Christmas : they 'd not been asked if they were banning the word Christmas to appease Muslims . <p> They had told the paper about their party plans and confirmed that they 'd always used secular language . <p> The reporter demanded @@8604057 <h> Harnessing the power of information to reduce crime &amp; victim <p> Minister of Justice3 May 2016 Speech Social Investment in the Criminal Justice System <p> Wellington <p> Harnessing the power of information to reduce crime &amp; victimisation <p> IntroductionGood morning and thank you Andrew Bridgman for the introduction and for hosting us at the Justice Centre this morning . <p> It 's fair to say that New Zealand has one of the most trusted justice systems in the world and is internationally very highly regarded . But that 's not something we should ever take for granted . <p> Our justice system underpins our confidence to go about our lives in peace , and helps to promote New Zealand as a civilised society , a well-functioning economy and trusted international nation . Indeed , the safety and security of the population is one of the most critical deliverables for any Government <p> There is much we can be rightly proud of . <p> Crime is at its lowest levels in more than 35 years . <p> Fewer young people are in our courts and youth crime is @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ government we 've taken significant steps to better protect the vulnerable in our society ; from the introduction of the Victims ' Offender Levy , our ground breaking laws to protect victims of cyber-bullying , better supporting young people to give their evidence , reductions in the number of grants of permanent name suppression and the appointment of the first ever Chief Victims Advisor to Government . <p> We are also undergoing a comprehensive cross Government overhaul of our approach to family and sexual violence in an effort to reduce the appalling effect these crimes have on society . <p> But it 's my view that the best thing we can do to support victims is to stop them from ever becoming one . <p> And in this regard our system is certainly not perfect . <p> We continue to face long-standing challenges . <p> For starters , the incidence and impact of crime is unevenly spread -- research has found that three per cent of adults experience 53 per cent of all crime . <p> It will come as no surprise to anyone here that Maori are over-represented in the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Maori are six times more like to end up in prison than non-Maori . <p> We are battling with a horrendously high rate of intergenerational family violence . <p> Despite falling crime rates , our imprisonment rate per capita is high by international standards . <p> In 2014/15 New Zealand imprisoned 194 people out of every 100,000 people . This placed us seventh-highest in the OECD just behind Mexico . We 're 27 per cent higher than Australia and 40 per cent higher than the UK . The current rate of imprisonment for Maori is much worse , standing at around 700 per 100,000 people . <p> While having the right people behind bars to protect the public is a good thing , it comes at a tremendous cost and we need to work hard to keep bringing the number of people committing these offences down . <p> None of these problems are new . Indeed they have challenged successive governments . <p> The fact of the matter is that we need to be prepared to think about things differently if we are going to get markedly different results . <p> @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ more information and data analytics capability than ever before , and the technology to bring it together to analyse and understand it with greater ease . <p> Armed with the power of that information , we have the ability to better understand the likely predictors of future behaviours , the risk indicators and the likelihood of the success of various interventions and investment in each case . <p> We now have the opportunity to use high quality data analytics to model how we can better reduce crime and victimisation , not just by what occurs once an offender comes into contact with the justice system , but long before that . And based on that to work with social sector colleagues right across Government to consider and track all the investments we 're making and the anticipated justice sector outcomes of those investments . <p> This is the theme I want to address this morning . <p> Social investmentI 'm sure by now you 've heard a lot about the Government 's Social Investment Approach being championed by the Deputy Prime Minister Bill English . <p> At its simplest Social Investment @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ rigorous and evidence-based investment practices to social services . <p> It means using information and technology to better understand the people who need public services , and what works , and then adjusting services accordingly . What is learnt through this process informs the next set of investment decisions . <p> More practically , we take this to mean targeting integrated services at those individuals , families and communities where dysfunction poses the highest long-term social and economic costs . <p> And it means understanding what services work . That is , evaluating which services , or which combinations of services , have been most-effective in reducing long-term social and economic costs , and for whom , and expanding or better-targeting those services . <p> It 's also about looking beyond the lens of any one agency and understanding the long term outcomes across a range of Government sectors and services from the investment in any part of the system . <p> Aim of applying the Investment Approach to Justice <p> This morning I want to outline for you how we 're planning to apply the Social Investment approach to the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ . <p> The approach will enable us to focus on better understanding a wide range of risk factors , and combination of those factors , that tell us likely outcomes if effective interventions are not put in place , and which interventions are in each case most likely to reduce future harm to society . <p> It 's about investing more effectively , and earlier , for the biggest achievable reduction in the burden of crime on society . <p> The Justice Sector has for some time been working hard to understand the impact of its various interventions to reduce crime and victimisation . From Police , to Courts to Corrections a tremendous effort has gone in to understanding and improving all our services on a daily basis and it 's incredibly encouraging to see the progress that has been , and continues to be made . We have a dedicated workforce across the justice sector that is committed to improving the lives of all New Zealanders . <p> This piece of work is about ensuring we give them further information based on the incredible data picture we now have , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ more importantly , it 's about enabling them to better co-ordinate with colleagues across areas like Health , Education , and Child Youth and Family , to try and prevent today 's vulnerable young people ever becoming offenders of the future . <p> The Investment Approach allows us to understand not only the positive impact interventions have on the outcomes in the sector investing , but right across Government . <p> So education successes are n't just thought of , and assessed by , the value of the education wins they deliver , but through a lens of understanding the likely reduction in future offending produced as a result . <p> So the effectiveness of drug and alcohol treatment can also be measured with reference to the reduction in the total harm burden to society of offending that may otherwise have occurred , and so on . <p> As well as the holistic impacts across the public sector , this approach is about ensuring organisations involved in crime prevention have access to high quality research , helping them to make better informed decisions about where to invest to make the biggest @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Investment Approach to Justice is based on four streams of work . <p> The first is about how we measure the burden that crime places on society , and how we will understand if our investments are reducing it . <p> In the welfare system they use a fiscal liability measure . <p> We are exploring a number of alternatives that may be more appropriate to crime . For example whether a harm based measure or an index based on a severity weighting of the offending could give a more meaningful picture . <p> The second stream of work is about building the statistical , actuarial models that will help us understand who in New Zealand is most at risk of future offending and victimisation . Again , while we know the high level picture , this is about giving us a much more granular analysis than ever before , allowing much greater targeting of interventions . <p> So for example , we know that in family violence one per cent of NZ adults suffer 62 per cent of family violence , meaning it has one of the highest re-victimisation rates @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ problems and substance abuse at age 15 , and leaving secondary school early , can be strong risk predictors of future family violence offending . We also know that a partner 's pregnancy or having a young child in the home , or a recent separation , or threat of it , can all be potential triggers for family violence . <p> We know those who offend at a young age can be significant contributors to future crime but we want to know which types of offending in particular are the greatest predictors of future recurrent offending . <p> The third stream of work is about understanding what works to reduce crime . Not just in the justice sector , but right across government and at all stages of the life-course as I 've mentioned . <p> For example do particular sentence types lead to statistically significant differences in re-offending when matched with comparable groups of offenders ? <p> Crime has been extensively studied over many years , but the research findings are not always easy for busy decision-makers to find and interpret . <p> An important part of the Investment @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ policy makers and researchers . <p> The final stream of work is about connecting these insights with decision-makers across the system and taking different decisions as a result . <p> The modelling that supports the Investment Approach is made possible thanks to the assembly of information on the Integrated Data Infrastructure , or IDI . <p> It is anonymised data brought together from various government agencies throughout the public sector that can be analysed to gain meaningful insights into people 's lives and better understand the relationship between crime and other social issues . <p> By applying smarter data analytics we 're able to point to real results that are reducing the burden of crime and reducing the number of victims . <p> The data also tells us how much crime would be prevented if we increased the level of investment in any particular area to ensure best use of the resource available . <p> Early insights from the Investment Approach <p> What the Investment Approach does is bring hard numbers to the long-held " truths " people working in the Justice sector have intuitively known . <p> We 've known @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ contact with the Justice System have drug and alcohol issues -- and that those drawn to a life of crime , usually start young . <p> And we also know that those who end up in prison have typically been known to government agencies for many years . <p> For example , three out of every four young prisoners was notified to Child , Youth and Family for a care and protection concern before they turned 15 years old . <p> Some of our early analysis found that for a cohort of people born in New Zealand in 1978 , 80 per cent of convictions went to those who were first convicted before the age of 20 . <p> This analysis also found that one in four of those born in 1978 has a criminal conviction . One in three men born in 1978 has a criminal conviction and one in two Maori and Pacific men has a conviction . <p> We 've always known that many people who interact with the Justice Systems have mental health issues but it 's been difficult for us to quantify the full extent or @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ is the first step of changing this . <p> For example , we 've found that while 11 per cent of the general public have used mental health services , this is dwarfed by the level of use of mental health issues of those in the criminal justice sector where mental health issues affect : <p> * 35 per cent of those proceeded against by the Police <p> * 40 per cent of those charged in court <p> * 51 per cent of those starting a community sentence . <p> Under our Investment Approach to Justice , we will continue to analyse mental health and explore both the severity of mental illness among people in the justice system and the prevalence of mental illness among particular types of offenders . <p> Another early piece of analysis has indicated that comparing a matched grouped of offenders across assault , drink driving and shoplifting offences , the imposition of a sentence of fines as opposed to community work led to lower amounts of re-offending and decreased likelihood of future benefit reliance . <p> Applying the Investment Approach to family violenceA key area where @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ of family violence . <p> The Ministerial Group on Family and Sexual Violence , which I co-chair with Social Development Minister Anne Tolley , is taking an investment approach to family violence to target support where it will make the biggest difference . <p> Each year we spend $1.4 billion on family violence . <p> We 've done a stocktake and our analysis shows that less than 10 per cent of that spend is focused on prevention . We are instead predominantly addressing the impacts of family violence , like prison costs and victim recovery . <p> It 's another example of how the system focuses on reacting to the worst criminal offending , rather than doing more to address it early on . <p> By taking a long-term view , the Investment Approach also offers the potential to disrupt inter-generational patterns of crime . <p> For example , we know that 7 per cent of children born in 1993 have been referred to CYFs because of exposure to family violence . <p> The outcomes for these children have been poor , and many have begun offending themselves , continuing the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ this specific group of children include : <p> * 44 per cent left school before the age of 17 &amp; 57 per cent did n't get NCEA level 2 <p> * Before the age of 19 , 52 per cent had received a main benefit <p> * By aged 21 it 's estimated that 15 per cent will have had a Corrections sentence and 5 per cent will have been in prison . <p> This information clearly shows that inter-generational patterns of offending are real , can be predicted using information on the IDI , and that exposure to family violence is a particularly important risk factor . <p> The Investment Approach is about taking the next step . <p> Having identified groups of people at risk of offending and victimisation in the future , we are now focusing on what we can do to disrupt the vicious circle of inter-generational offending today . <p> Doing more of what works <p> The Investment Approach is not only about predicting risk , it is about understanding what services work and for whom . <p> The Government spends billions of dollars every year @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ for much for that spending , we simply do n't know what is making a difference . <p> The problem is that where we are supporting interventions that do n't work , what we are doing is taking the time and energy and reducing the resilience of the people we are trying to help . <p> Through the Investment Approach , we are building the capability to understand how the interventions we fund are changing outcomes for the people we are trying to help . <p> As information about services effectiveness becomes available , we 'll be increasingly using that knowledge to inform where to invest next . <p> It 's this system of feedback that means the Investment Approach can deliver sustained pressure for improvement in public services . <p> Funding commitment <p> The Government has so far invested $2 million of additional funding from the Justice Sector Fund to fund the Investment Approach to Justice alongside the core work of officials from across the sector . <p> PwC has also been brought on board to assist the Ministry of Justice on work stream two and the development of the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ on building the statistical models on the Integrated Data Infrastructure that will predict life-course patterns of offending and victimisation . In turn , these models will allow us to understand concentrations of risk in the population , simulate the effect of changes in sentencing policy on crime , and forecast future crime . <p> ConclusionIt 's an incredible privilege being the Justice Minister . But success for me will ultimately be about how we can better target our resources to reduce the incidence of crime in our society . <p> The criminal justice sector is about holding people to account but also working to prevent people ever being victimised . <p> The Investment Approach is about addressing the core issues to stop crime happening , reduce harm and prevent victims from being victimised in the first place . <p> The point here is that when CYFs are involved in a neglect case -- that is an opportunity for future crime to be prevented . <p> When a primary teacher is dealing with a kid who is truanting -- that is an opportunity for future crime to be prevented . <p> When @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ an opportunity for future crime to be prevented . <p> Under the Investment Approach , data and better use of it is helping identify the areas of greatest need where the Government can best target our efforts , and do more of what works . <p> Ultimately , this will make a significant difference in helping to reduce the burden of crime on New Zealanders -- both as taxpayers and as victims . <h> Parliament Today : <p> Last year , the world began spending more money on weapons again , for the first time since 2011 ... New Zealand belongs to a region -- Asia and Oceania -- where military spending rose sharply in 2015 , by 5.4 per cent . @@8604805 <h> SST Demands a Royal Commission of Inquiry <p> SST Demands a Royal Commission of Inquiry After Another Child Beaten to Death <p> 2nd May 2016 <p> " The most precious jewels you will ever wear around your neck are the arms of a child " Author unknown . <p> Moko Rangitoheriri was a defenceless 3 year old child who was battered to death over a lengthy period of time by the very people who he relied upon for his survival . <p> Tania Shailer , 26 , and David William Haerewa , 43 , both pleaded guilty after charges of murder were questionably reduced to manslaughter in Rotorua High Court . <p> Both defendants supported each other 's attacks on the victim and the " culture of violence " that occurred within the home . The viciousness increased over time . Shailer was observed by a witness biting the victim and Haerewa admitted to continually attacking Moko . He said it became routine to pick on the victim because he did n't want him around him . <p> " A ruptured bowel , fractured ribs and eyes @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ on this innocent child over a lengthy period of time is premeditated murder not manslaughter " Guthrie <p> The question must be asked as to how the Police , who do a wonderful job in our community , can lay a charge of murder against these offenders only to have a plea bargain of manslaughter negotiated by the defence and agreed to by the Crown Prosecutor . <p> Murder is defined as when the offender has been so reckless and means to cause such bodily injury to one person even though he or she does not mean to hurt the person killed as is clearly written in our Law . <p> " This case is Lilybing , Nia Glassie and the Kahui twins all rolled into one ! The assault was premeditated and prolonged fitting all the criteria for murder . The fact the Crown have accepted a guilty plea of manslaughter smacks of collusion of the highest degree and is symbolic of New Zealand 's horrific level of child abuse . " Garth McVicar . <p> Scott , backed by Garth McVicar is calling on the Governor General , Sir @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ this child 's death and to find out how a child 's life can be worth so little and how the most serious crime of murder can be reduced to such a shallow charge of manslaughter . <h> Parliament Today : <p> Last year , the world began spending more money on weapons again , for the first time since 2011 ... @@8605538 <h> Death by Torture is Definitely Not Manslaughter <p> This is not justice New Zealand style ; in fact it is a dirty back room deal of the worst degree . " McVicar . <p> The Sensible Sentencing Trust has vowed to do all in its power to ensure the death of three year old Moko Rangitoheriri is not in vain . <p> Trust Founder , Garth McVicar said that SST would turn the toddlers death into a catalyst for change.Three year old Moko Rangitoheriri was left in the " care " of his minders , Tania Shailer and David Haerewa . Both administered systematic torture because Haerewa said he did n't like Moko being around him . <p> Moko 's torture started two months before his death , gradually escalating in seriousness and violence , ending in him suffering violent treatment so bad that the pathologist was unable to confirm which of the many life threatening injuries Moko sustained had in fact killed him . <p> Haewera locked the child in the bathroom for hours . That was before stomping on his stomach so hard it ruptured @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ stomach and body . The pain this defenceless little boy endured would have been excruciating . <p> Add to the stomping , repetitive beating around the head until his brain was swollen and bleeding , the lung injuries , abrasions all over his body and human bite marks on his cheeks arms and legs.Finally the toddler was unable to speak or walk , and still his tormentors did not seek medical assistance . When they did it was too late , and Moko died what would have been an agonising death five hours after being admitted to Taupo Hospital . <p> " McVicar said this baby was tortured to death , and now his death is being aggravated by the Crown accepting a guilty plea of manslaughter to what was clearly a case of murder where recklessness as to the result of injuries inflicted is deemed to be intent to kill . <p> " This case is Lilybing , Nia Glassie and Kahui twins all rolled into one ! The assaults were premeditated and prolonged , fitting all the criteria for murder . The fact the Crown has accepted a guilty @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ and is symbolic of New Zealand 's horrific level of child abuse . " <p> " These two killers have escaped their just desserts because of the murky practice of plea bargaining , which has been formalized in the last couple of years . Once it was only the defence who could try and persuade the Crown to reduce charges -- now either side can initiate such discussions . Whereas prosecutors were once very reluctant to do deals , now they are initiating them ! <p> This is not justice New Zealand style , in fact it is a dirty back room deal of the worst degree . " said McVicar . <p> " Twenty five years ago charges were often laid in the alternative . Until the latest murky system was formalized , defendants such as these two would have almost certainly been charged with murder , with the jury being instructed that they could find them guilty of manslaughter instead . That decision has now been removed from jurors in favour of shady deals done in some lawyer 's office , with the public never being told how such @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ McVicar said . <p> " Anne Tolley has recently announced a ' root and branch ' reform of CYF . We also need to drag the shady system of plea bargaining into the light , and get rid of it . Plea bargaining used to be something we saw on American television . Now it is our reality happening every day in a courthouse near you . We say this system must end , and end now . Juries should decide the level of killers ' culpability , not a cabal of lawyers meeting behind closed doors who aim to dispose of cases as quickly as possible , however much injustice that involves . @@8605546 <p> We have visa waiver arrangements with more than 50 countries to make it easier and more attractive for people to visit . <p> We also want people to invest here . <p> Almost $100 billion in foreign capital was invested here last year . <p> That creates jobs and businesses and provides funding to get infrastructure projects off the ground . <p> The new $200 million Park Hyatt in Auckland and the $35 million Sofitel in Wellington will create jobs during construction and when the hotels are up and running . <p> They also add to our tourism infrastructure . <p> Christchurch has been chosen as a location for one of only seven international " xones " established by Vodafone . <p> These are purpose built spaces to support startups and encourage innovation . <p> And the Queenstown Resort College is educating young New Zealanders in Otago and Northland . <p> These are just a few examples . <p> New Zealand has benefited immensely from investment which has come from all around the world . <p> Those investors see our growing economy , booming tourism , strong @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ continue to stimulate growth . <p> They see our strong track record and growth forecasts of around 3 per cent on average over the next few years . <p> Ultimately these investors chose New Zealand because they see opportunity here . <p> They believe in our future . <p> So do I. <p> This Government knows we can compete and we will succeed . <p> Because history has shown we can . <p> No one owes us a living , we must earn it . <p> Trade , investment , migration -- they make New Zealand a better place . <p> That 's why agreements like TPP are so important -- and why New Zealand pushed so hard for it . <p> I want to spend some time today talking about the TPP and how it fits into the Government 's wider programme of building ties across Asia and the Pacific . <p> The farmers , growers and manufacturers of 1960s New Zealand could never have imagined the opportunities we are creating today . <p> We are exceptionally well-placed to take advantage of those and I want to lead a country @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ that backs itself on the world stage . <p> As I 've said many times , we wo n't get rich selling things to 4.5 million New Zealanders . <p> But we could by selling to 4.5 billion people overseas . <p> That 's why we support strong international connections . <p> And why we focus so strongly on making it as easy as possible for our businesses to succeed through good public policy . <p> We understand the benefits that free trade agreements deliver and the opportunities they offer . <p> I back our farmers , our manufacturers , our ICT companies and in fact all our export industries to succeed . <p> If we can get an equal crack at world markets , we 're up there with the best in the world . <p> That opportunity is what free trade is about for New Zealand . <p> And the TPP is our most significant free trade deal to date . <p> It will open up access to 800 million people around the world . <p> On current trade volumes , tariff savings will reach $274 million a year @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ our exporters an equal opportunity to sell their goods and services in markets including the world 's first and third largest -- the US and Japan . <p> We often talk about the overall benefits to New Zealand , but this deal matters to individual businesses and workers in every region of the country . <p> The orchardist in Hawkes Bay , the winegrower in Marlborough , the dairy farmer in Waikato and the IT provider in Auckland all stand to benefit . <p> Removing those tariffs and non-tariff barriers will make it easier for them to compete . <p> In total , the deal is forecast to add at least $2.7 billion a year to New Zealand 's GDP by 2030 . <p> And as we saw from our FTA with China , these agreements have a tendency to exceed our expectations . <p> Since we became the first developed country to sign an FTA with China in 2008 our exports to that country have almost tripled . <p> It is now our first or second-largest trading partner , depending on what you count and when you count it . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ billion a year and our goal is $30 billion by 2030 . <p> Opposition to TPP tends to flare up then fall away again pretty quickly because nothing much of substance is ever raised . <p> And most of what is in TPP is also in our existing China and Korea FTAs , neither of which has killed off democracy , wrecked the environment , led to wholesale obesity , or whatever the current anti-TPP message is . <p> Trade agreements are always a matter of give and take . <p> But New Zealand is already an open , transparent and trade-friendly country , which means only a fraction of TPP 's obligations will require us to change what we already do now . <p> The only significant cost comes from extending New Zealand 's copyright period from 50 to 70 years . <p> This cost -- in terms of foregone savings on books , films , music and other works -- increases gradually over more than 20 years and averages around $55 million a year over the very long term . <p> Some people think that cost is overstated . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ proposals to do with PHARMAC and intellectual property that were raised earlier in the negotiations were not included in the final deal . <p> We will continue to honour the Crown 's Treaty of Waitangi obligations . <p> Parliament remains sovereign and we will continue to legislate in the best interest of New Zealanders . No one dictates to New Zealand . <p> The next step is to introduce the TPP legislation to Parliament next week . <p> This follows a series of meetings around the country to better inform the public , as well as the release of the TPP text and the opportunity for the public to submit on it . <p> The Trans-Pacific Partnership Agreement Amendment Bill will make the legislative changes needed to ratify TPP . <p> This includes applying preferential tariff rates , intellectual property obligations , and increasing the investment screening thresholds for significant business assets . <p> After introduction the TPP Bill will go through the standard parliamentary process , including further opportunities for public submissions to the Select Committee . <p> These amendments will only come into effect once TPP enters into force @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ early in 2018 . <p> And we wo n't stop with the TPP . <p> We also hope to upgrade our existing FTA with China and we 're in negotiations with the " While it essential that environmental risks are adequately managed and mitigated through a comprehensive resource consent process , fish farming offers potential for further benefits . " <p> The report recommended the council directs that a tender should proceed for space to farm fish within the specially designated 300 hectare zone . <p> Several years ago there was strong interest in farming kingfish and hapuku in the region . But due to the global financial crisis interest in fish farming waned . <p> Now staff have been advised of renewed interest in farming fish , with the single undisclosed party involved looking at occupying space in the outer Firth of Thames that has been zoned for fish farming . Because of this renewed interest , staff put the report to the committee . <p> Space available in the zone would be released for development after a tender process . That process would take into account key factors of a bid such as environmental management practices , economic benefit , and any monetary contribution to council and central Government . <p> Successful tenderers would need to apply to the council for a resource consent . The report noted that any application for a resource consent to farm fish would have to be accompanied by a comprehensive site specific assessment of potential environmental effects . @@8607047 <h> Thoughts of our young , progressive thinkers <p> This work brings together the thoughts of young , progressive Kiwi thinkers , whose lived experience is the wonderland of Rogernomics and Ruthenasia , reviewer Victor Billot writes . <p> THE INTERREGNUM : RETHINKING NEW ZEALANDMorgan Godfery ( ed ) BWB Texts <p> Reviewed by VICTOR BILLOT <p> Having spent my adult life as a socialist , it sometimes feels like being carried out to sea on the ebbing tide of history . <p> Things have changed . In the early 1990s , you could feel a visceral anger at the fourth Labour Government , followed by National PM Jim Bolger 's promised ' ' decent society ' ' , which combined mass unemployment , benefit cuts and a raft of changes that smashed the New Zealand working class over the head , courtesy of then Minister of Finance Ruth Richardson . Yet there was resistance , of a kind . <p> Scroll forward a couple of decades , and the outcomes of that era have been hard-baked into New Zealand society . <p> The Clark Labour Government made @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ the blandly incremental John Key does n't need to force through controversial policies , as the hard yards have already been done . All he needs to do is wait , as time is on his side . The young have grown up in a nation where the dominant values and organising principles are those of acquisitive individualism . The ' ' money men ' ' have won . Or have they ? <p> I was intrigued to read this concise collection of essays that bring together the thoughts of young , progressive New Zealand thinkers , whose lived experience is the wonderland of Rogernomics and Ruthenasia . <p> As a 40-something orbiting in the far reaches of the mortgage belt , I wondered what was going through the minds of people who have come of age in the 21st century . The answer is quite a lot . <p> This collection takes its title ( and implied theme ) from the Italian Marxist Antonio Gramsci , who wrote of European society in the turbulent 1930s that : ' ' The crisis consists precisely in the fact the old is dying @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ interregnum a great variety of morbid symptoms appear . ' ' <p> Morgan Godfery was an inspired choice for editor . His introductory overview is not lacking in intellectual firepower , bouncing between Marx and Facebook , Foucault and child poverty , and urging the need to remain ' ' determined to resist melancholy - the feeling that progress is better mourned than desired - and replace it with optimism , politics and love . ' ' <p> Courtney Sina Meredith opens the volume with a short but radiant poem , appropriately linking past and future . The brevity of the following essays means they are readable , but by no means shallow , as well as being largely free of jargon and pretentiousness . <p> Andrew Dean uses the Eleanor Catton controversy - the semi-hysterical response to her critical comments of the New Zealand Government at an overseas conference in 2015 - as a starting point to interrogate how dissent and non-conformity is controlled , discouraged and limited in our society . <p> Wilbur Townsend examines the future of work and advocates automated technology , a universal income , and @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ ' ' socialism that can emulate capitalist dynamism without emulating its callous set of values ' ' . <p> Trade unionist Edward Miller recounts how global capitalism undermines any serious attempt to deal with the climate change crisis , and Chloe King has an insightful essay on the way that precarious work and a punitive welfare regime is used to divide and disenfranchise a younger generation . <p> Lamia Imam is a New Zealander who has spent much of her life in Bangladesh , and writes on the effects of living in a globalised world . Former Green MP Holly Walker provides a personal account of the difficulties of being a political representative in a society where family , community and personal wellbeing are undervalued . <p> A couple of the essays I was unconvinced by , but it is essential that a book of this kind draws on as wide a range of thought and reflection as possible . <p> As such , it is appropriate for it to conclude with an original piece by Max Harris who seeks out a ' ' politics of love ' ' . Far @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ wider values to be brought to bear on the ' ' stale and sterile ' ' language of the neoliberal political establishment and its agendas . <p> The Interregnum is a must-read political and social commentary for 2016 . <p> - Victor Billot is editor of The Maritimes , the magazine of the Maritime Union . 
@@8621116 <h> ' Progress ' on water quality has been in the wrong direction <p> MARNIE PRICKETT <p> Last updated 05:00 , May 4 2016 <p> Stacy Squires <p> A sign at Lake Forsyth warns of toxic algae . <p> OPINION : It was hard to miss the incongruity of the title of David Caygill 's opinion piece , " We are making progress on water quality " last week , next to a photo of Lake Forsyth 's shores smothered in algae so toxic a teaspoon would kill a child . <p> In one sense , Caygill , Deputy Chairperson of Environment Canterbury ( ECan ) , is right . We are making progress . Sadly , we are progressing toward a situation where blooms such the one that has so far killed 30 sheep and slowly choked fish and eels to death at Lake Forsyth become more frequent . <p> We are making progress toward a country where our kids can only be told about when their parents used to swim in the local rivers as they drive across the plains on a hot summer day ; @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ " Health Risk " signs have been in place for so long that lichen partially obscures the words . <p> It 's important to note that Caygill did not say , " We have improved water quality " . He would n't be so bold . It would deny the lived experience of Cantabrians and expose too starkly the failure of Ecan and central government to protect what is of vital importance to us all , clean safe water . <p> ? Instead , Caygill hides behind years of backwards processes . He goes to great pains to outline the hearings , the committees , the appeals , the panels that have led to ... his personal feelings of confidence that water quality will improve . An incredible amount of public resource and time has been spent making sure David Caygill does n't share the despair of the rest of us . <p> Ironically , it was the disregard , disrespect and disestablishment of public process that was the very thing that got him his job . <p> Meanwhile , research published last month by scientists from University of Waikato , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ increase in the distribution and extent of Phormidium-dominated proliferations in New Zealand 's rivers over the last decade " . Phormidium is the toxic algae . Their research includes work on the Selwyn and Opihi Rivers and concludes that this increase is toxic algae likely driven by changes in the last decade to " flow regimes ( due to water abstraction or climate change ) , run-off of nutrients , sediment and other contaminants , and habitat modification . " <p> Dr. Alison Dewes , veterinarian and agroecologist , who has been called as an expert witness in four Canterbury regional and sub-regional plan changes , is astounded by Caygill 's confidence . She notes that the Canterbury Water Management Strategy has set its bottom line for nitrates at a standard that is nine times higher than the standard at which problem algal blooms can be triggered . Algal blooms similar to what we are witnessing at Lake Forsyth . Yes , David , this has been our progress on water quality . <p> Caygill 's misguided confidence , like John Key 's incredible recent statement on TV One that New @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ this country back . What 's clear is we are using 19th and 20th Century thinking to solve 21st Century problems . <p> Caygill believes large scale irrigation will make us more resilient to climate change . The reality is the opposite : it will lock us in to an input-heavy model that is n't nimble enough to cope with the increasingly unpredictable weather patterns my generation will see and have to deal with for the rest of our lives ; in the same way that poor stormwater and wastewater treatment systems built last century have made it extremely challenging for us to protect and improve urban streams and rivers . <p> We now have what has been described as a " once-in-a-generation " opportunity for freshwater . Let us not , as ECan is suggesting , bend or scrap freshwater legislation so that we may continue to build progress on pollution . <p> Let 's establish healthy people , rivers , lakes and groundwater as our priority in law . From this foundation , we can encourage the creativity and innovation that New Zealanders are known for to develop smart @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ support life instead of endanger it . We have the knowledge in New Zealand to be a shining light for generations facing environmental challenges on a scale we have not witnessed before . <p> Marnie Prickett is an Agricultural Sciences student at Massey University and the spokesperson for Choose Clean Water NZ.

@@8626877 <p> Imagine this : alongside the nutritional details listing sugar , fat , calorie and salt content , the labelling on your food shows the volume of greenhouse gases released in its production . <p> Or this : Fonterra , rather than being just a dairy company , is reinvented as a protein company , with an array of plant-based products like oat milk and nut burgers . Or that the supermarket snack-food aisle offers high-protein insect bars , roasted fava beans and other products developed by clever entrepreneurs alert to the demands of the carbon-conscious consumer . <p> It sounds fanciful , but the point is that when it comes to greenhouse gas emissions , food matters . Around half of New Zealand 's emissions are from agriculture -- mainly methane from ruminants such as dairy cows , beef cattle , sheep and deer . Since 1990 , our farm emissions have increased 14% , largely because of the expansion of dairying , and are predicted to rise a further 30% by 2050 , according to the Royal Society report on moving to a low-carbon economy . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ almost doubled since 1961 and are estimated to have been 10-12% of total greenhouse gas emissions between 2000 and 2010 . The world 's population is predicted to reach nine billion by the middle of the century , and with more people in developing nations demanding a higher-protein diet , farm emissions are set to soar . <p> Debate is raging internationally about the need to cut meat and dairy consumption -- both for climate-saving and health reasons . A 2015 report by UK 's Chatham House said the average person in industrialised countries is eating twice as much meat as is deemed healthy , and concluded that curbing demand for animal protein was crucial to keeping global temperature rise below 2 ? C. <p> Economist Lord Nicholas Stern once predicted that meat-eating will eventually become as socially unacceptable as drink-driving because of the impact of animal emissions on the climate . <p> Former Intergovernmental Panel on Climate change chairman Rajendra Pachauri has also called for people to have at least one meat-free day a week if they want to make an effective personal contribution to preventing dangerous climate change . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , ANYONE ? <p> It 's challenging stuff for a country such as New Zealand , which makes its living by exporting animal protein to the world . Should we diversify our agricultural expertise away from dairy and meat production and into legumes , nuts and other plant proteins ? As it happens , New Zealand is particularly well-suited to growing nutritious fava beans , a hectare of which produces about 90 times as much usable protein as beef and about twice as much protein as cows ' milk , says Nick Pyke , chief executive of the Foundation for Arable Research . <p> But there 's not much interest in , or funding for , research on the potential role of high-protein plants such as fava beans in New Zealand . Pyke and others have looked into products such as fava " nuts " -- roasted beans -- but were unable to generate any excitement among food manufacturers . <p> " It 's difficult , because the products that we can grow really high yields of are the products that people in the world do n't pay much for ... @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ sorts of products in New Zealand , but the volume of roasted fava beans the world is going to eat is pretty minimal unless there is a change in diet . We looked at the question ' what if every kid took a pack of these to school every day ? ' and the reality is it was a couple of farmers ' production . And so there is no reason a company will set up to do that . " <p> Andy Reisinger . <h> HAVING THE STOMACH FOR IT <p> For now , the focus of efforts to reduce farm emissions is on techniques to lower the volume of methane produced from animals ' rumens and nitrous oxide released from soil . Trials are under way on low-emission forages , selective breeding for lower-emission animals , and vaccines and chemical inhibitors to counteract or knock back the methane-producing microbes in the animals ' rumens . <p> Even the most promising technologies are still a long way from commercial application , but the Royal Society reports that short-term trials indicate it may be possible to reduce livestock methane by 30% @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ reduction of 5-10% could be achieved " in the long run " . But the effects of such technologies are uncertain , because their use would rely on gaining international acceptance and adoption by farmers who , under the present rules of the emissions trading scheme , are exempt from any carbon price that might encourage them to do so . <p> The Royal Society says modelling indicates that even with improved farm efficiency and the development of emission-reducing technology , New Zealand 's agricultural emissions are unlikely to fall below 1990 levels by 2050 and could be substantially above . This raises questions about whether constraints on agricultural production could be justified . <p> Andy Reisinger , deputy director of the New Zealand Agricultural Greenhouse Gas Centre and lead author of the chapter on agricultural emissions in the Royal Society report , says New Zealand is presented with two distinct " discourses " on agricultural emissions . In terms of emissions per kilogram of protein , New Zealand farmers are among the world 's most efficient producers , raising the question : " Is New Zealand 's primary obligation to @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ possible , given that there is relentless global demand for the products , or whether it is more the ethical principle of ' do no harm ' , and therefore ' no you ca n't keep on increasing your emissions if the world has a problem with climate change ' . " <p> There 's no simple answer . As he says , science can " inform but not settle " that debate .

@@8629185 <h> Watershed agreement to end water bans <p> A watershed agreement announced today in Hawke 's Bay could be the start to ending irrigation bans across New Zealand . <p> The agreement , between a group of Twyford landowners in Hastings and the Hawke 's Bay Regional Council , comes three years after a total water ban sparked grower outcry and public protests . <p> Under the agreement landowners in catchment area have agreed to " give to gain " by signing over their existing individual water rights to become part of a group stewardship model under a global consent . <p> Led by growers and supported by Horticulture New Zealand and Pipfruit New Zealand , the agreement is being heralded as an exciting step forward for leading future water irrigation management in New Zealand . <p> Twyford Co-operative chairman and HortNZ representative Jerf van Beek said this " watershed agreement " provides landowners , growers and farmers across the Twyford catchment with certainty and reliability of water , even in severe Hawke 's Bay Regional Council resource management group manager Iain Maxwell said growers can now tap @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ it 's needed , as determined by the grower group . <p> " It is a great environmental and economic solution that meets high expectations of the public on managing water rights within the limits outlined by the Resource Management Act , without trade-offs or compromises . <p> " Our agreement could lead to a change in the way irrigators and councils work through irrigation bans throughout New Zealand , " Mr Maxwell said . <p> Pipfruit New Zealand chief executive Alan Pollard said it showed how the irrigator and regulator could work together for a practical and pragmatic " We now have an industry template for others to follow , " he said . <p> Three years ago the Hawke 's Bay Regional Council imposed a total water ban across Twyford when rivers reached crisis levels , sparking unprecedented grower outcry and public protest against the decision . <p> The Twyford Co-operative catchment , west of Hastings , covers 1292 hectares of fertile growing land for pipfruit , grapes and cropping . It has 166 water consents in the unconfined and semi confined Heretaunga aquifer . <p> Twyford grower and @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ of the first to sign into the global consent , said uniting to share water would protect all landowners . <p> Mr Bayley owns and leases over 200ha of confined , semi-confined and unconfined land across the Heretaunga Plains growing fruit and vegetables . <p> " Why should my neighbour be penalised for a lack of water when I have more than enough water under my resource consent , " Mr Bayley said . <p> " The 2012/13 drought brought everything to a head , Twyford had lots of water in the catchment , but under the old consenting process we could n't get to it , in the end we were limited to the river flows which enforced a total water ban . <p> " We had to come together to find a workable , sustainable solution . There is still work to be done , but we 've come along way , " said Mr Bayley . <p> Senior planner and principal of Cheal consultants Cameron Drury , who developed the global consent framework and resource consent application , said it was a paradigm shift for both irrigator and @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ regions . <p> " It gives the Twyford co-operative the tools and responsibility to manage existing water rights within the limits of the Resource Management Act , " Mr Drury said . <p> Mr van Beek said more than half the land area is now globalised into the semi- confined zone and he was confident of gaining full uptake after the 2016 harvest season . <h> Parliament Today : <p> Last year , the world began spending more money on weapons again , for the first time since 2011 ... @@8646323 <h> A savvy solution for biking even in Sweden 's weather <p> A Swedish man has built a recumbent bicycle with an outer shell that resembles a car , creating a pleasant riding experience during his country 's bitter winters . <p> While the popularity of bicycle commuting has grown globally due to factors such as climate change and urban gridlock , cycling in bad weather has remained unpleasant . Many bike commuters pick other modes of transportation during winter and on rainy days . <p> But mechanical engineer Mikael Kjellman was determined to bicycle year-round to his job in Ostersund , about 563 kilometres north of Stockholm , where winter temperatures rarely get above freezing . <p> So Kjellman built the PodRide , which shields him from inclement weather with a windshield and canvas enclosure . There 's a heater built into the electric bicycle , which is programmed to automatically warm his bicycle one hour before he leaves the office each evening . The PodRide has a cute appearance , with large wheels . <p> It 's less than a metre across and seats one person @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ to carry things . The bicycle 's electric assist motor will reach speeds of 24 kilometres per hour , a limit Kjellman set to follow local laws . ( A cyclist could pedal faster if desired . ) The motor can add power and make pedalling easier . <p> Mikael Kjellman with his PodRide , which shields him from inclement weather with a windshield and canvas enclosure . PHOTO : MIKAEL KJELLMAN/WASHINGTON POST <p> The 42-year-old has made the 11km commute in the PodRide for a year . The only times he ca n't use it are when more than 10 centimetres of snow are on the ground . <p> Kjellman rides the PodRide in bike lanes and on local streets with limited traffic . He owns a car but generally uses it only on weekend trips to his cabin in the mountains . <p> Kjellman drew so many smiles and positive feedback as he rode the PodRide that he launched an online campaign to raise money to sell kits so anyone could build their own PodRide , he said . In two weeks on the crowdfunding platform Indiegogo , he @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ . An accompanying YouTube video has been watched almost 3 million times . Those donating to his campaign are essentially putting down a down payment on their kit .
